The new chemical, microbiological, genetic testing methods have become the standard. Not every institution and especially every area will be able to do everything at a high international level, in high, certified form. For example, in the field of the University of Innsbruck, the Hygiene Institute and my Federal Institute, we have an excellent specialist with Mr. Univ.-Prof. Dierich and his focus in the field of Enterobacteria and other bacteria. We have an excellent focus in the field of salmonella in Graz in my Bundesanstalt. We focus on chemical research in Vienna, Salzburg and Linz. Although the debate on the praise or approval of Commissioner Byrne has now dominated the debate – it has also been expressed in interlocutory calls – I would like to point out that the involvement of human medical epidemiology by the federal bacteriological-seriological research institutes is also a step that enables the examination of plant and animal products to be carried out from the field, through the finishing of the plants, to the effect on the human organism. Ladies and gentlemen! I would like to ask you to take note of the debate over the last few days on limit values, on compliance with limit values, on crossing limit values and on food safety as a whole: The limit values have not been lowered in a single case, but otherwise they have always been lowered. I therefore think it inappropriate when a campaign is launched with the mixture of a result where the limit values have been exceeded, with 14, 15 other results, where residues below the limit thresholds have been found, to call on food safety as a whole. I also believe that the Food Agency and the research networks between human medicine, veterinary medicine, microbiology and chemistry will be a useful addition to things that we do not yet know fully and comprehensively. However, I think it is actually curious that, on the basis of a single work and on the basis of reports that have been four or five years old, a debate is being held in advance of today's debate at the expense of agriculture, at the expense of trade, and especially at the expense of consumers, which is neither up-to-date nor appropriate in this form. I ask you, ladies and gentlemen, to pay attention to the limits of truth in the discussion, and not to insure people for populist reasons, in cases where the limit values are clearly lower and where results from neighbouring countries, Argentina and America, are being discussed in order to insure consumers and producers in Austria. I can add something: Yesterday, my officers from Wellington came back from the WTO conference. It was also about the Code Commission. ParlaMint-AT_2002-02-27-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00094_d7e17147	Mr President. Federal Minister! High house! Even more official samples have been planned for 2001. Some 13,000 samples were commissioned by private authorities and paid by them. That means that today around a quarter of all food samples are private samples. These revenue from the examination of the private samples are important funds for the investigative bodies. With around 17 million schillings, around €1.2 million, they contribute to the cost optimization of the research institutes. Of course, Austrian producers will continue to submit samples to the research institutes, i.e. in future to the Food Safety Agency. The examination of the samples serves the producers for their own safety – complicated investigations can only be carried out by the Agency because the necessary equipment in the companies is not at all foreseen – but also to control the competitors, because often producers also have samples from competitors controlled. What can the Austrian consumer rightly expect from the new agency? He can expect a pooling of forces to take place. It can expect greater cost efficiency to be achieved, and it can also expect a clear analysis of the samples, particularly in the case of complex problems. In the past, it has often been the case that research institutes have come to different results through different equipment, and this is not favourable for Austrian consumers or producers.

This control in the waste sector was really a central point for us. The same applies to the uniform state of the art in terms of treatment principles. One point that we also had to discuss for a very long time, although this is actually a matter of course, is the possibility for advisory members not only to have insight into opinions in the future, but also in all the documents and data that are the basis of these opinions. That was also an important point for us, particularly in terms of transparency in this whole area. There are also some points that we are not satisfied with. This has already crystallized in the negotiations, and I also said it in committee. I've already mentioned the party position. I also see that there will be a general reduction of neighbourhood rights, as was the case with the EIA law. We are not happy with this, but we feel that because of the fact that the Environmental Procuratorate has some sort of party position, we have been able to make a positive change. Of course, we wanted to talk about the issue of all product-related regulations, such as the target regulation. Our objective was actually to bring about a primary committee obligation for the change – for example, the packaging target regulation – so that it cannot happen as last time that five minutes before the achievement of the objective simply changes the regulation. At this point we couldn't get through, and I think it's a big manko because I and many other consumers really get angry every day when they go shopping in the supermarket and desperately try to find a multi-way glass bottle. Another important point would have been a special antitrust provision within the framework of the ARA, within the framework of this monopoly system. We could not reach an agreement either. Ladies and gentlemen! In summary, however, we have come to the conclusion that we have still been able to negotiate some very important points into the AWG, and that is why we will agree.

In the Government Declaration, the government has committed itself to creating a modern waste management law, and it has also been necessary to implement a relevant EU directive. – But it wasn’t that simple from the beginning: The first draft, which was sent out in September last year, was partly due to strong resistance from industry and waste management. It is therefore gratifying that it has now succeeded in taking all these objections into account, without leaving behind the objective of meeting the requirements of the waste management. It has succeeded in creating a modern law that ensures EU conformity with waste prevention as a priority that is more sustainable and does not, however, create a deterioration and additional burden on the economy. Although the law is a little more extensive than the old AWG, it is nevertheless clearer, easier to read and better summarized. One of the main problems was – as is already done today – the contradictory concept of waste in EU law, which was to be taken into account in the law and which, in my opinion, is in stark contradiction with a circular economy, because products resulting from manufacturing are immediately considered waste with all related obligations – including reporting obligations. [[Intrusion of the Abg. May. Kogler.]] Even if a material is still a valuable group product, it would have been waste anyway, without a disposal plan. However, we have succeeded in ensuring that a circular economy is possible and that internal waste can be used further. And in my opinion, this is really the best waste prevention. The new data pool is very important – as has already been said today. In this respect too, although there have also been some resistance from the waste management sector, it has succeeded in establishing a committee to ensure that there is no significant new burden on the economy. This new data pool is of course very important for the recovery of waste streams for both hazardous and non-hazardous waste. Business and plant law has also been better regulated. There are efficient procedures through concentration of procedures which do not require approval for certain waste producers, such as medical practices or offices. With the use of the Federal Government's competence in the field of national competence, it has definitely succeeded in establishing a uniformity, because, as has been said, there were usually up to nine different regulations in the federal states; no company dealing with waste management could really understand why, if he worked beyond the federal borders, he had to deal with different legal bases. Ladies and gentlemen! This new waste management law represents waste prevention, a sensible cost-benefit principle, environmental sustainability, administrative simplification and EU conformity. Another point of the Government Convention was therefore done positively. A further sentence on the request of the Greens or SPÖ concerning the mobile phone fee system: In our opinion, this request is not necessary because there is already an electronic scrap regulation, there is also a battery regulation. Mobile phones, which are operated with batteries to be disposed of, are therefore, of course, covered by this battery regulation; that is, there is already a sufficient legal framework. In addition, a European Electroalt Equipment Directive is already in preparation. We will therefore not be able to give our approval to these two requests.
